READING 01

A Small Device, a Real Problem

A student engineering team wanted to build a portable air monitor. Their aim was simple: give families direct information about the air around them. An expensive instrument was already available in the university, but it was too bulky for daily use. The team needed a compact gadget that could fit in a coat pocket.

Their first version had a serious flaw. The receiver sometimes lost messages because of electrical interference. A magnetic part was too close to the wires. The students moved it and made another modification. They also changed the composition of the case, using aluminium for its frame and a flexible cover around the edges. The cover had to withstand repeated falls without damaging the delicate parts within it.

A supplier offered cheaper parts, but the team did not assume that a low price meant good value. They asked the manufacturer for test records. A professional engineer then helped them assemble the device and execute a test program. They put each measurement beside a reading from the university's instrument. The results were consistent, except when the air was very wet. Water from condensation collected on a cold surface and changed the readings.

The team wrote a comparative report about two designs. The quantitative section compared numbers; the qualitative section described users' experiences. One user could not read the small screen. Another wanted a tablet application with larger writing. Such feedback helped the students simplify the controls and enhance the screen's brightness so older users could read it more easily.

A trial followed. The device would automatically send its readings to a laptop. Users could insert a memory card to save an extra copy. A light showed when it was time to charge the battery. The team wrote an accessible tutorial and explained routine maintenance. They also provided a patch for a small software error.

The project taught a useful lesson. Innovative ideas need realism, careful investigation, and practical knowhow. A device is valuable when people are able to use it, replace worn parts, and trust its readings. Clever design alone cannot guarantee that.

READING 02

Who Should Decide How a Town Changes?

A small town planned to turn an empty factory into a public centre. The local government hoped the renovation would create jobs. Yet the first proposal caused a dispute: some families feared that higher rents would force them to leave their neighbourhood.

The planning department arranged a formal hearing. Every resident could explain an opinion. An influential business owner wanted shops; a school wanted classrooms. Neither group had universal support. An independent consultative group suggested a collaborative process in which residents could compare multiple plans.

At first, the discussion was tense. People suspected that the result had already been decided. The committee therefore published its spending, the price of each acquisition, and the terms of the building's lease. This transparency increased its credibility. Residents could see who was accountable for each decision.

The committee also conducted a poll. It asked about needs instead of trying to sway voters with attractive pictures. The chief concern was affordable space. A vacant floor could provide classrooms, while shops would occupy the ground floor. A small grant would support evening classes. The remainder of the cost would come from shop rent.

Members had to justify their choices. They could not impose arbitrary rules or let a single investor dominate the process. They agreed to regulate rents and curb sudden increases. Any compensatory payments to families who had to move would be listed separately. The proposed compensation was open to enquiry.

The final accord did not satisfy everybody. Some members continued to differ about the size of the shops. Nevertheless, the plan had a clear framework, and residents had a real opportunity to influence it. The committee's declaration promised monthly reports and a review after a year.

Public collaboration takes time. But the imposition of new rules without explanation can undermine trust. A useful plan must consider both the buildings and the people who belong to the community.

READING 03

Reading Beyond a Word List

At the start of the semester, an English teacher asked her students a question: could they understand a short text without translating every word? Many could recite a long vocabulary list, yet they became anxious when familiar words appeared in a new setting.

The teacher offered a sensible explanation. A list gives only part of a word's meaning. Readers also need to notice its neighbours and its role in a sentence. An adverb, for example, can change how readers interpret an action. A semi-colon can connect two closely related ideas. Neither should be ignored.

For practice, the class read a paragraph about a school garden. Students first wrote a summary. Then they compared their answers and asked for clarification where necessary. The purpose was to distinguish between what the writer had said and what the reader had merely imagined. An erroneous guess was useful if students could explain its origin and correct it.

A dictionary helped, but constant checking caused disruption. The teacher suggested a short pause after each section. Students could then gather unfamiliar expressions and consult their notes. They learned to pronounce difficult words and spell them, but perfect speech was not required before reading could continue.

One exercise involved a retelling of an episode from a child's diary. The class checked the coherence of each account. Did the ideas follow a clear order? Did the retelling remain faithful to the original? A good translation needed the same care. Students could translate a sentence into simple Chinese without copying its English word order.

The teacher was particularly interested in fluency, but she did not confuse it with speed. Fast reading without understanding was pointless. She asked students to acquire useful habits through periodic practice. Learning would become more rewarding when they could follow an entire argument.

There was no magic formula. Persistence, concentration, and honest recognition of weaknesses mattered more than a perfect daily score. Students should strive to improve, while using short reviews to consolidate what they knew. Such reinforcement made progress easier to notice and gave them a reason to persist.

READING 04

A Farm That Learns from Its Land

A farming village faced longer dry periods and less predictable rain. These climatic changes did not affect every field equally. Some land remained fertile; other fields lost water quickly. The farmers needed to understand the variation before buying new equipment.

They invited a researcher to compare the soil. She examined its organic material and nutrient content. In one field, a deficiency of a key nutrient explained the poor growth. In another, coarse sand allowed water to escape too quickly. The problem was different, so the answer had to be different too.

The village changed how it would irrigate its crops. Workers laid a pipe beside each row of plants and used automatic controls to supply water only when needed. This let them apply a new method without replacing all their equipment. This reduced wasteful watering. Covering the ground with straw helped keep it moist. Farmers could still plough selected fields, but they avoided turning all the soil every year.

The researcher also compared a variety of bean that grew quickly with another variety that used less water. A useful gene could influence growth, she explained, but no plant was free from the limits of its environment. Good seeds could not replace sensible care. Her report included a projection of water demand, but she stressed that it was an estimate, not a promise.

At harvest time, children helped collect a ripe pea crop from the school garden. A gardener showed them how to transplant young plants without damaging their roots. In the backyard, a cock walked past a goose, while a pigeon rested on the roof. The children watched eggs hatch and learned why clean water mattered to animals as well as crops.

The village did not become rich overnight. However, productivity improved, and farmers spent less on water. A yearly review helped them anticipate problems and compare results. They hoped to reap lasting benefits from careful utilization of the land. The change was gradual, but it supported both their livelihood and the next generation's needs.

READING 05

Making Space for Wildlife

A group of students travelled to a northeastern forest to explore how a new road might affect wildlife. From an elevation of eight hundred metres, they could see an expansive valley. A stream passed through pine trees, and a narrow trail led towards a cave.

An environmentalist explained that a road can cause fragmentation of animal habitats. A forest could look intact from above while becoming several disconnected pieces on the ground. A fox could cross a small road, but a wide expressway presents a much greater hazard. A tunnel under the road could help animals move between the two sides.

The team studied several species. A bat used the cave, and a colony of birds lived by the water. A migrant could arrive in one seasonal period and leave in another. A count made only in summer would therefore miss part of the story. The students had to document both occasional sightings and regular movement.

At a visitor centre, a film showed a tigress searching for food. It was fascinating, but the guide warned against treating wild animals as tame pets. People should not chase them or try to touch them. Human intrusion could scare animals away from places they needed. The team also examined reports of illegal hunting and wildlife trafficking.

Later, a fisherman from the coast described how boat noise could affect a dolphin. The example helped students see a broader pattern: protection was about conditions for life, not just attractive photographs. A cliff, a muddy bank, or a patch of bare ground could each serve a different animal.

The team's final recommendation was to preserve routes between habitats and reduce unnecessary disturbance. This would not eliminate every problem, but it could reduce vulnerability. The disappearance of wildlife was not always inevitable. Careful action could protect the places where animals fed, rested, and raised their young.

READING 06

A Museum of Ordinary Lives

The old museum had recently changed its main exhibition. It still displayed an imperial flag and a painting of a king, but it now asked visitors to look beyond royalty. What had life been like for a farmer, a factory worker, or a slave?

A scholar of sociology helped prepare the exhibition. Her research showed how the region began to industrialize. An old forge became a machine workshop, and later a large textile business opened nearby. A display of iron tools stood beside cotton cloth. Visitors could compare a wealthy family's silver plates with a worker's wooden bowl.

One document described a farming commune. Another recorded a rebellion against forced labour. The exhibition did not disguise the misery of the poor or celebrate colonial domination. It showed how a powerful lord could own land while other people had little control over their lives.

Political change appeared through personal objects. A proclamation announced the founding of a republic. Beside it stood a monument to people who had died in the struggle. Letters revealed disagreements among supporters of republican government. One writer feared a new dictator would simply replace the old ruler. Another questioned whether nationalism would unite people or deepen division.

The museum also displayed a ragged coat, a bony child's photograph, and a letter from a distant relative. These objects gave history a human scale. A woman's inheritance included a pearl necklace, but the heiress later donated it to support schooling. Her generosity was described through records rather than family myth.

The curator hoped to enrich public understanding. Visitors could admire a beautiful object while asking who had made it and at what cost. An object's beauty did not erase an unfair past. The exhibition's ultimate purpose was to dignify ordinary lives and show how a historic change could reach every household.

READING 07

An Investigation Is Not a Verdict

A newspaper received a message from an informant. It alleged corruption in a public building project. A dealer had supposedly paid officials to purchase unsafe equipment. The accusation was serious, but an editor reminded the reporters that a rumour was not evidence.

They began with records. Several payments had no clear explanation, and a safety certificate was missing. The firm's executive issued a denial. He said a clerk had made an accidental error. Reporters had to establish whether the problem was an oversight or intentional concealment.

A later police raid found messages suggesting bribery. Investigators also discovered a separate gang connected to a warehouse robbery. The newspaper kept the cases separate because a connection between people did not prove that every offense was part of the same plan.

At an interview, a former employee agreed to confess that he had changed figures. His confession was telling, but reporters still checked it against the records. They would not accuse another person merely because the employee named them. Nor would they deny evidence that weakened their original theory.

The case eventually reached a jury. One defendant had been released on bail while waiting. The court considered which documents were valid and whether each person had acted knowingly. A witness's seeming certainty could mislead; confident speech was not proof of accuracy. The final ruling depended on evidence, not public anger.

The editor removed offensive descriptions and abusive comments from the newspaper's website. Some readers called the alleged conduct scandalous and shameful, but this did not justify threats or torture. An innocent person could be harmed by careless reporting. Even a guilty person had to be treated according to the law.

Responsible reporting must challenge deceit and deception without becoming another source of unfairness. The staff's ethics required them to correct mistakes, protect privacy, and resist pressure. Their task was to help people understand what was known, what was disputable, and what remained uncertain.

READING 08

A Business That Can Keep Its Promises

An entrepreneur opened a small repair company after several years as a trainee. She was not a millionaire. Her savings were limited, and the first workshop was little more than a room behind a shop. For local families, a working refrigerator was a necessity, but a new refrigerator was expensive. She wanted to offer repairs they could afford.

She decided to specialize in household equipment. The business would sell repaired products as well as provide services. A brand name and a trademark helped customers recognize it, but managerial competence mattered more than a bright sign. She needed to control each expense and remain solvent. Otherwise, one bad month could leave the business broke.

The owner made prices clear. She would specify the cost of parts, labour, and carriage before accepting a job. Payment dates were negotiable, but an overdue bill could not be ignored forever. When customers continued to owe money, she sent polite reminders rather than angry threats. A teller at the bank helped her understand the account records.

Growth brought new choices. An overseas buyer offered a large order. The first shipment would travel as sea freight, and payment would arrive in another currency. The owner checked the cost of export services and the risk of a fluctuation in exchange rates. An attractive order could become ruinous if these costs were ignored.

She also changed how staff worked. Clear administrative rules helped her administrate the business, but excessive restrictive procedures wasted time. She gave a capable employee a promotion and invited staff input on tools and schedules. Their coordination improved, creating a more cohesive team.

The owner did not try to maximize profit on every repair. A modest discount for repeat customers built trust. Loyal customers became the firm's greatest asset. Admittedly, the business was still small. Yet its outlook was promising because it could keep the commitment behind its advertising. Lasting success required more than a rapid increase in sales.

READING 09

What a Community Kitchen Really Needs

A community cafeteria wanted to make its menu both affordable and appealing. Customers could dine there at noon or buy supper to take home. The cook's preference was simple food prepared with care, not a lengthy list of expensive dishes.

Breakfast included toast, eggs, and fruit. Lunch offered rice, vegetables, and a meat dish. On Fridays, customers could choose steak or fish. A crab dish was an occasional treat. The cook prepared a mild sauce and left pepper on the tables. People who disliked salty food could season their own meals.

A volunteer measured flour for bread while another added a spoonful of sugar to lemonade. A bowl of peanut sauce stood apart with a clear label. The cook used a knife to cut fruit and checked whether its flesh was fresh. Raw food and cooked food had separate containers. Refrigeration was part of the daily work, and the refrigerator temperature was recorded.

The kitchen had simple cleaning rules. Staff used soap for their hands and a fresh towel where needed. A broom removed dry dirt, and a mop cleaned the floor. Food wrapping went into the correct bin. No litter could remain beside the stove. The manager checked the toilet as carefully as the dining area.

Avoiding waste required judgement. Food could spoil, bread could become mouldy, and milk could turn sour. Staff did not use strong flavours to hide an odorous product that seemed unsafe. Nor did they assume that every preservative was bad. They asked a food specialist to explain ingredients and storage requirements instead of repeating advertising claims.

The kitchen's training covered nutrition as well as cost. It helped volunteers lay plates on the tables, divide portions evenly, and answer even picky customers with politeness. At a nearby cafe, a food vendor donated vegetables each week. Good meals depended on this quiet cooperation as much as on a talented cook.

READING 10

A Clinic Built Around People

A new clinic opened in an inland county. Its medical team included a surgeon, nurses, and visiting doctors. The reception was designed to be quiet and clear, so a sick person would not have to ask several times where to go.

The clinic's first concern was prevention rather than impressive equipment. A preventive education program discussed infectious diseases and answered questions about AIDS. The staff avoided fear-based messages. An outbreak could produce panic, especially when information was unclear. Notices distinguished the incidence of illness from its severity: how often something happened was different from how serious it was.

One patient, a factory worker, felt weak and feverish. Another reported pain after an accident and worried about a broken rib. Doctors examined each person's condition before deciding on care. They could hospitalize someone who needed closer attention or discharge a patient when appropriate. A visitor who became dizzy and pale received help instead of being told simply to wait.

The team knew that unfamiliar words could heighten anxiety. A doctor explained what the lung and liver did using simple pictures. A visiting specialist described organ transplantation, without presenting it as an easy answer to every problem. A prescription listed the medicine clearly, and patients could ask what each drug was for. Treatment decisions belonged to trained staff, not online guesses.

Care also meant protecting dignity. Staff asked permission before examining a scar or helping someone bathe a wound. They understood that it could take time to heal. A patient's gut feeling that something was wrong deserved attention, even when the explanation was uncertain.

The clinic could not remove every burden. Yet a kind greeting, a clear explanation, and respect for gender and personal circumstances could relieve distress. Indifference made people wait helplessly for an answer. Good service replaced that feeling with a chance to be heard. Patients were thankful for staff who treated them as people, not case numbers.

READING 11

When a Journey Changes Its Plan

A student used her vacation to visit a scenic mountain region. She bought a single ticket for an express train and checked her passport before leaving. Her suitcase was light, but she still preferred a seat near the carriage door so she could manage her luggage.

The journey began at a crowded platform. A shuttle bus had brought passengers from the airport, where an airline had announced a cancellation. A railway worker helped them find an alternative route. Some would travel north, others south. The student was eager to leave, but she did not hurry through the gate.

At her destination, a hotel manager explained that room occupancy was high because of a festival. She offered a temporary room at a small resort farther up the valley. The route was less convenient but offered a magnificent view. The student agreed and travelled by jeep to a wooden cabin.

The next morning was misty and chilly. A guide explained that conditions could deteriorate quickly at high altitude. A snowy section made one path perilous. The guide chose to postpone the longer walk. Being adventurous did not mean ignoring risk.

On a shorter route, the student saw a mule carrying supplies and watched clouds drift above the southern hills. At sunset, the sea became visible in the distance. She found the view superb, although the trip was different from her original plan.

Later, the guide described how tourism supported families but also created pressure. During a rainy season, visitors sometimes demanded journeys that were unsafe. Considering the conditions, a change of plan was preferable to a dangerous adventure. The student agreed. A memorable trip needed some flexibility, not constant control over every step.

READING 12

Learning to Train, Learning to Stop

The university sports club welcomed beginners in May. Its coach said that a good workout should match a person's ability. Looking muscular was not the only goal. Students could choose running, cycling, or swimming. A winter group also offered skiing lessons.

At the stadium, each contestant wore a numbered badge. The first practice was not a contest to discover the strongest student. It was a chance to learn basic motion and coordination. Beginners completed one lap and then rested. The coach observed their breathing rate and how they felt, rather than praising anyone who pushed through pain.

One teenager was terribly nervous about looking weak. He wanted to prove that he was manly and could beat every rival. He ran too fast, hurt his ankle, and felt faint. The coach stopped the session. A strain was not a useful accomplishment, she explained, and refusing help was not bravery.

The next week, the student returned with a different attitude. He checked each shoe and tied every lace carefully. He practised keeping his body upright and reducing unnecessary movement. When a ball began to bounce towards him, he stepped backward instead of rushing into another player. Small improvements gave him real satisfaction.

The coach used encouraging feedback to motivate the group. A victorious team should congratulate its opponents and remain modest. A triumphant celebration was welcome, but jealousy and contempt damaged the club. Students learned to behave with respect even when disappointed.

By the ninth session, the group had improved its attendance and confidence. Youthful energy was valuable, but so were moderation and rest. Sweat alone did not prove progress. The coach hoped that students would find joy in movement and continue the pastime long after the competition ended.

READING 13

A Stage Made by Its Community

A small theatre planned a musical for its forthcoming festival. The plot followed a young woman who left home and later returned to help her family. The heroine was played by a shy student. She had little experience in acting but was willing to practise.

The production brought together a guitarist, a jazz singer, and a retired actor. An instrumental opening led into a song with a rhythmic beat. The director encouraged natural expression and some spontaneity. A performance could be accurate yet dull if every facial movement looked forced.

Behind the stage, volunteers made each costume. A silky purple skirt was sewn from donated cloth. Old cushions provided stuffing for a stage sofa. A volunteer used scissors to cut fabric while another added makeup. A pair of loose pants belonged to a comic character, and a voluminous coat helped the actor look older.

The director rejected an advertisement that called the young performer “sexy.” It did not fit the role and reduced her work to her appearance. The replacement poster described her artistic choices. A large banner invited families to attend, while a bold heading explained the ticket arrangements.

During rehearsal, an actor playing the heroine's father called her “my darling.” The line sounded awkward at first. A simple nod and a warmer voice made it believable. The cast learned that romantic scenes and family scenes both needed trust. Their mutual respect became highly valuable when mistakes happened.

On opening night, the climax came when the family met again. The ending was hopeful but not perfect: the characters still had problems, yet they could face them together. A lover of theatre praised the group's work. It was no famous masterpiece, but it gave the audience enjoyment and gave the performers a lasting sense of fellowship.

READING 14

Why Old Stories Still Matter

At a library event, a novelist and a poet discussed why readers return to old stories. The novelist said that fiction can create an imaginative space for difficult questions. A legendary hero or a fairy could be unreal, yet the feelings in a story can be genuine.

Her latest book followed an adventurer through a desert. A cowboy helped him find water, and a village lass warned him about a false guide. The word “lass,” she explained to a listener, suited the older voice of the story. She did not use it as the ordinary word for every young woman in modern conversation.

The hero had to enter an underground passage beneath a ruined temple. A giant guarded a door, and a monstrous shadow moved along the wall. A marvellous event saved him: a stone bird became alive and led him to safety. The story's narration was designed to fascinate readers, not teach them facts about the natural world.

The poet asked about the hero's motive. Was he seeking money, freedom, or enlightenment? The novelist answered that his thirst for recognition changed during the journey. At first he wanted supreme power; later he learned to value mercy. A merciful act towards an enemy became more important than winning a fight.

Readers could see the same proverb differently depending on their experience. A quote about courage might comfort one reader and trouble another. That was part of the fascination of literature. Its singularity often came from a familiar situation seen through an unfamiliar voice.

The writers also discussed revision. They would edit a page to remove empty language and strengthen its main idea. A derivative story might copy another writer's surface style without understanding its purpose. A stronger creation offered a unique perspective. A story did not need to be true to convey something truthful about people.

READING 15

The Sky Is Not a Simple Picture

A university astronomer gave a public lecture about space. On the screen, a spherical model represented a planet. It moved along an orbit while a smaller body appeared to revolve around it. The scientist warned that the model was a tool for thought, not a complete copy of the sky.

To understand planetary movement, students needed both observation and calculation. A small mistake could change a predicted position. They used geometry to study spatial relationships and checked their arithmetic, including multiplication. A cube helped them compare three-dimensional shapes with flat pictures. Two lines that looked parallel on a screen could represent objects at very different distances.

The astronomer described orbital travel and the thrust needed to change a spacecraft's movement. Engineers had to consider fuel, mass, and maximum load. A spacecraft might carry only a small cargo, but even that could have an immense cost. A successful launch was the beginning of the work, not its end.

Another display showed a beam passing through glass and a crystal surface. An atom could not be understood simply as a tiny version of that large model on the table. The atomic world required different ideas. A vacuum chamber and a container of gas let visitors compare two carefully controlled settings.

Questions soon turned to relativity and nuclear power. The astronomer explained the limits of a short public talk. Such topics involved conceptual difficulties that a few exciting images could not remove. Approximate numbers were useful for a first explanation, but the audience should not confuse them with precise results.

A child asked whether space travel would soon become as ordinary as aviation. The astronomer did not make a dramatic promise. A space shuttle on a poster could appear simple, yet its design involved thousands of decisions. The most useful insight that evening was that science welcomes curiosity while asking for evidence. Wonder starts a question; careful work helps answer it.

READING 16

A Science Room Full of Questions

The school science room welcomed every inquisitive pupil. Its teacher wanted experiments to develop an analytical habit, not simply entertain. Before each activity, students wrote what they expected and what would count as evidence against that expectation.

One demonstration involved wax. As it became warm, it began to melt. Students compared the solid material with a fluid form and watched a small air bubble rise. They recorded the intensity of the heat and the time required. A circular mark on the table showed where equipment should stand. Safety rules did not permit students to dip a finger into the hot material.

Next, the teacher used a model to explain how an acid and a base could neutralize each other. The activity was primarily about a chemical compound, not a kitchen recipe. Students were told that some materials could be poisonous. They must never taste an unknown substance with their tongue or shake a closed container without instructions.

A geology display stood on the upper shelf. A fossil was evidence of earlier life, while pictures of a volcano showed a changing landscape. The teacher explained that, geologically, a quiet place today could have a very active past. Maps of continental movement encouraged students to compare rocks on opposite shores. The lesson offered an introduction to evolutionary change without pretending that one object could explain all of it.

At another table, students used a computer mouse to enlarge images. They checked the index of a reference book and wrote a citation for an idea they used. The teacher explained the derivation of a scientific term, then asked them to describe it in ordinary language. A technical word was useful only when they understood it.

By the end, the room was still full of questions. That was an ideal outcome. A clever answer was welcome, but a compelling explanation needed more than confidence. The teacher praised modesty about what remained unknown. The students could possibly be wrong; that was a reason to check, not a reason to stop.

READING 17

Preparing Before the River Rises

A township beside a river had suffered a disastrous flood. Its inhabitants remembered the terror of water entering their homes. This year, the fire marshal and a rescue commander planned an exercise before the raging summer storms arrived.

The first task was to marshal supplies. The rescue corps checked boats, radios, and every blanket. Workers used a spade to clear drains and a hammer to repair a broken fence. They had to reinforce a river wall, because rising water could penetrate damaged sections and wash away the earth underneath.

During the exercise, volunteers learned how to inflate a rescue boat and fasten a safety buckle. A trained operator checked the engine's horsepower and its working limits. On land, drivers tested each brake. Equipment failure could necessitate a last-minute change of route, so teams prepared alternatives.

When the alarm sounded, a loud message gave a clear direction. Participants moved across a bridge towards higher ground. Cars had to crawl through one narrow section. Nobody was allowed to block the emergency entrance. The team practised helping an elderly person who began to shiver in the cold wind.

The review showed a pressing problem: one neighbourhood had no reliable transport. The town arranged a provision of extra buses and updated its personnel list. Volunteers learned where to greet displaced families, how to clothe children whose clothes were wet, and when to request further help.

The exercise could not remove every danger. A sudden surge in water level might still bring severe damage. But preparation helped people cope and reduced confusion. Amidst an emergency, calm cooperation was more useful than dramatic promises. The team took utmost care to make its plan understandable, because a plan that nobody could follow would not rescue anyone.

READING 18

More Than a Line on a CV

A university dean invited graduates to discuss their first year at work. One graduate had found a job quickly; another was still unemployed. The event did not pretend that every career followed the same path. Its purpose was to help students make decisions without letting rejection destroy their motivation.

A graduate brought a worn briefcase and a pile of early applications. “I was sure good grades would be enough,” he said. “Then I learned that a company also wanted evidence of what I could accomplish.” A certificate could record study, but it did not automatically demonstrate practical ability.

The university therefore offered students a chance to enroll in a project course. The group would consist of six members with different strengths. Each person had to perform a clear task and describe their own contribution. An industrious student might work many hours but still need help choosing priorities. A resourceful teammate could find an answer when the first plan failed.

The course also taught networking. Students learned to contact people with a specific request rather than ask strangers to solve everything. A short seminar could introduce an acquaintance who later became a helpful ally. However, connections could not replace skill or honest work.

Students were encouraged to visualize a useful project, not a perfect future. The first attempt could fail. That did not make the person a failure. A good team could boost confidence by identifying a weakness, finding a next action, and checking whether it worked. A combination of effort and feedback was stronger than effort alone.

At the end, the dean asked students not to sacrifice all leisure for an ideal image of success. Rest helped preserve vigor and vitality. The attainment of a skill took time. Ultimately, a useful education should support the fulfillment of realistic goals and help students become more confident. The most exemplary graduate did not always have the most impressive title.

READING 19

The Work of Staying Close

After her parents' divorce, a young woman moved to a different city. She kept correspondence with her grandmother but rarely visited. Her grandmother was fond of handwritten letters, so the woman bought blue ink and an envelope instead of sending another short message.

At first she wrote only cheerful news. She was secretive about loneliness and tried to conceal the emptiness she sometimes felt. Her grandmother answered with an earnest letter about her own youth. She had also experienced grief and uncertainty. That honesty helped the younger woman feel less alone.

A family reunion was finally arranged. The grandmother's grandchildren arrived with flowers, and her niece brought a cake. An old photograph helped them discuss their ancestry. It showed a grandfather in work clothing, smiling beneath a tree. Time had made the picture fade, but the memories remained strong.

The younger woman had been a rebellious child. She remembered angry arguments and felt regretful. Her grandmother was forgiving without pretending that nothing had happened. “You can say sorry and still learn from it,” she said. A tear ran down the woman's face, and her grandmother touched her chin gently.

Later, they sat under a reading lamp. A little boy climbed onto his grandfather's lap and tried to amuse him with a joke. The grandfather laughed, then gave a tired sigh. “We can finish the story tonight,” he said. The boy pointed at a stain on his grandfather's shirt and asked whether it came from the cake.

Nothing about the evening was grand. A slipper was under a chair, and unfinished washing waited by the door. Yet these familiar details created a sense of peace. The woman felt deeply obliged to the people who had welcomed her. To reunite did not mean repairing every disagreement in a single evening. It meant giving the relationship another chance and showing gratitude through attention.

Back to contents
READING 20

Learning to Read Political Arguments

A college class studied politics by comparing arguments about public services. The teacher invited students to examine a capitalist approach alongside a Marxist account of ownership. The debate was not a test of obedience. Students could contend that an argument was convincing, but they had to explain why.

One group considered a state facing conflict with a neighbour. A delegation met an ambassador to discuss an alliance. Supporters said it would prevent attack; critics feared it could compel the country to enter another war. The agreement contained a way to leave, offering each country an out if conditions changed. The class discussed how international rules might restrict a government's freedom to invade another country.

A second group examined a historical crisis involving a military defection and a violent riot. Students asked how newspapers had described it. Were their accounts based on records or pure speculation? A classified report was not necessarily reliable merely because it had been secret. Equally, public disgust at an event did not prove every accusation true.

The teacher then raised a question of morality. Could the same action be acceptable in one setting and wrong in another? Students considered religion, tradition, and law. They distinguished a social convention from a reasoned judgement. A conventional view could be reasonable, but its popularity did not establish its truth.

To avoid false comparisons, the class examined equivalence. Two claims might look similar while resting on very different evidence. Treating them as equal could create a paradox: an apparently fair debate might actually hide an important difference. Whether a particular comparison was useful remained arguable. The students had to look at its limits.

At the end, the teacher asked them to propose a fair rule for discussion. They agreed not to preach, insult others, or demand agreement for the sake of harmony. A participant should be free to utter an unpopular opinion, but should also accept questions. Firstly, identify the claim; next, examine the evidence; lastly, decide how strongly the evidence supports it. This approach could make even a topical argument less confusing.

Back to contents
READING 21

A Room Where Newcomers Belong

A community charity opened an evening school in an old hall. Its students were mostly adults, including an immigrant who had arrived with little knowledge of the local language. A guardian could bring a child, so parents did not always need to find childcare before attending.

The school's establishment depended on a donation from a small winery and support from an orphanage. Volunteers would decorate the room, hang notices, and prepare books. They were conscientious, but the organiser warned against praising them as tireless. Everyone needed rest, and services should not be dependent on endless unpaid work.

Classes covered literacy, basic numbers, and everyday communication. Some students had left secondary school early. A teacher tried to acquaint them with forms used by banks and employers. A skillful volunteer showed how to write a clear message. Students could ask a question without fearing embarrassment.

Cultural activities helped people relax. One evening, a newcomer practised using chopsticks to pick up small pieces of food. Another student shared a song in a regional variant of a familiar language. A woman explained why she chose to pray before a meal. Nobody had to conform to another person's beliefs to be welcome.

The organisers took racist comments seriously. Any attempt to victimize a student would receive a firm response. Respect was not a favour given to people who already fitted in. Acceptance meant allowing newcomers to keep their identity while joining a common activity.

After roughly three months, the school had enough interest to triple its evening classes. The sum of all fees remained small: students paid only a little per lesson. The organiser's solemn promise was printed at the entrance: “We hereby welcome every learner who is ready to respect others.” That promise showed the depth of the volunteers' devotion to the school.

READING 22

A Forum Needs More Than Rules

A student ran an online forum for repair advice. At first, people shared pleasant messages and answered practical questions. Then gossip began to creep into the discussions. A casual remark about a seller turned into a nationwide argument after a popular account repeated it.

The student could overhear classmates arguing about the post at lunch. Some were mad at the seller; others felt astonishment at how quickly the story spread. One user wrote, “Incredible! Everyone knows this is true.” Yet the message offered no evidence. The student realised that reliance on repetition could make a lie seem convincing.

He added a rule: accusations must include a source. Users could refer to documents, but should not post private addresses or intrude into someone's personal life. It was not permissible to threaten a seller. Serious abuse could result in disciplinary action, including a decision to suspend an account. Legal responsibility was another issue; users should not assume they could never be held liable for harmful posts.

One angry user tried to hijack every discussion. “Excellent service,” he wrote ironically under a photograph of a broken tool. Sarcasm alone was not evidence. The student asked him to explain the fault instead of attacking other users. Another member replied, “Listen, mister, you cannot just order us around.” The student understood the anger but kept the rule the same for everyone.

He also learned to sort posts by category. A complaint, an advertisement, and a technical question needed different treatment. Printing a warning in large letters did not make it reliable. Nor did a well-known account automatically deserve trust. The source mattered more than the number of followers.

The changes did not please everyone. Some users would hate any restriction. Maybe a few would leave. Still, the student found a useful realization: nothing required him to choose between complete silence and nonsense. He could allow disagreement while asking for reasons. It was advisable to remind users of that purpose before an argument became personal.

Back to contents
READING 23

Building a Bridge People Can Trust

A small city on the eastern side of a river needed a new bridge. The old crossing had once carried only light carts. Now buses and trucks used it daily. Engineers did not hesitate to recommend replacement after finding damage in the wood and stone supports.

The new design used segmental construction: separate sections would be joined to form the bridge. This approach could decrease the time spent working above the river. It also provided a stimulus for local training. Workers would learn new methods rather than simply repeat the usual tasks.

Before the main work began, the engineer climbed to the top of a temporary tower. She checked the depth of the water and the space available for boats to sail past. A worker wiped his brow as they discussed how to move a heavy section. The crane's grip had to remain secure, and sharp objects must not pierce the protective covers.

The project manager refused to deem a drawing safe merely because it looked neat. The builders had to possess the necessary skills and provide records. Their report needed to feature clear evidence, namely test results and measurements. An inspector would personally examine the joined sections and await confirmation before allowing traffic.

A delay would doubtless annoy some drivers. Furthermore, costs could mount. But opening early without proper checks could render the bridge unsafe. The city decided that the continuation of testing mattered more than a convenient opening date.

The bridge eventually opened, and buses crossed without a long wait. The change was remarkable, but engineers avoided claims of permanence. Weather and use would still wear the structure down. A stable bridge needed regular care. This was the profound lesson of the project: construction ends, but responsibility continues.

Back to contents
READING 24

What Cleaner Air Would Actually Mean

A school in a northern city began an air-quality project after several smoggy mornings. A grey haze hung above the streets. Some students wanted the government to shut every factory immediately. Others argued that this would kill plans for new jobs without solving every cause of the problem.

Their teacher asked them to consider where pollution could originate. Traffic, heating, and industry all needed examination. The auto industry was part of the discussion, but it was not the only part. A factory on another continent could operate under different conditions, so copying a foreign solution without checking the local situation was risky.

The students decided to initiate a survey. They compared records from one week with those from another. A slightly lower reading did not prove that the air had become safe. A single number was only indicative of conditions at a particular place and time. They needed to understand the extent of the change.

The teacher asked them to remove unsupported claims from the report. “Fresh air will arrive tomorrow” was a wish, not a forecast. “This problem will simply vanish” was equally weak. In the meantime, the students could examine indoor conditions. A man smoking a cigar near an open doorway reminded them that pollution could move inside as well as remain outdoors.

Two proposals emerged: announce an ambitious goal, or explain practical actions with clear responsibilities. The class preferred the latter. It was less dramatic but more useful. The knotty question was how to support both workers and cleaner production. A good answer would require cooperation and could not rely on instinct alone.

By the end, the students remained hopeful. Cleaner air would not come from idly waiting, but neither would angry certainty solve everything. They had plenty of questions and no perfect answer. The project's main virtue was a change in their pursuit of solutions: they now asked what evidence a plan needed before believing it would succeed.

Back to contents
READING 25

A Competition That Changed Its Purpose

A student club held a mathematics Olympiad to raise interest in problem solving. It was a rare occasion for younger students to meet university volunteers. The organisers wanted participants to enjoy thinking, not feel that a low score measured their worth.

One volunteer noticed a boy sitting apart. He was sleepy after staying up late, and his attention began to wander. The volunteer did not discourage him with a lecture. Instead, she asked which problem he found interesting. To her surprise, he explained a difficult idea clearly when he no longer felt watched.

“Did you really solve that yourself?” another child asked. The boy said he had, then explained his reasoning himself. The volunteer checked every step. He had narrowly missed the answer because of a small error. Everyone except one student agreed after the explanation; the remaining question led to a useful discussion rather than an argument.

The organisers had prepared prizes, but the volunteers involved wanted more time for questions. Their personal experience suggested that competition could motivate some children and frighten others. Generally, students who asked questions learned more from the event than those who simply waited for marks. Oftentimes, the most helpful question sounded simple.

The club changed the afternoon plan. Volunteers would stand at each side of the room to offer help, and participants could readily ask for an explanation. Students were occasionally allowed to work in pairs. The event's friendly atmosphere mattered more than preserving every original rule.

It was regrettable that several children had already left feeling unsuccessful. The club sent them invitations to a free follow-up lesson. One good thing had come from the mistake: the team learned that the least visible benefit could still matter greatly. A quiet child willing to ask another question might not seem impressive on a results sheet. Yet that small act could convey hope. There was no doubt that the next event should make room for it.

The results were consistent, except when the air was very wet. Water from condensation collected on a cold surface and changed the readings.
n.价值；价值观v.重视；给…定价是

The lesson offered an introduction to evolutionary change without pretending that one object could explain all of it.
adj.上面的；上部的是

专业搭配补核：第 23 篇 segmental construction 采用“由部分组成的”义项，文内以 separate sections 解释；参照 FHWA 对分段桥梁技术的介绍。

③ 文章中文参考译文

以下按英文段落顺序意译，供第一遍阅读完成后核对理解。

01 · A Small Device, a Real Problem

一个学生工程团队想制作便携式空气监测仪，目标是让家庭直接了解周围的空气情况。大学已有昂贵的仪器，但日常使用过于笨重。他们需要能放进口袋的小装置。

初版存在严重缺陷：磁性部件离导线过近，电气干扰使接收器偶尔丢失信息。学生调整了部件位置，修改外壳结构，采用铝框架和柔韧的边缘保护层，使精密部件能经受反复跌落。

面对廉价零件，他们没有认定价格低就意味着划算，而是向制造商索取测试记录。在专业工程师的帮助下，他们组装设备、执行测试，并将每次测量与校内仪器的读数比较。结果通常一致，但潮湿时冷表面凝结的水会影响读数。

比较报告分别以定量部分比较数字、以定性部分描述用户体验。用户提出屏幕文字太小、需要大字平板应用等问题。这些反馈帮助团队简化控制方式，让老年人更容易看清屏幕。

试验中，设备自动向笔记本电脑发送读数，也可插卡保存额外副本。指示灯提醒充电。团队提供了易懂的教程、日常保养说明和软件补丁。

项目说明，创新需要务实、调查和实际诀窍。设备能被人使用、维修，并提供可信读数，才有价值；巧妙设计本身并不能保证这些。

返回对应英文

02 · Who Should Decide How a Town Changes?

一座小镇计划将空置工厂改成公共中心，借翻新创造就业。但初步方案引发争议：一些家庭担心租金上涨会迫使他们离开社区。

规划部门举行正式听证会，让居民表达意见。商人希望开店，学校希望增加教室，没有哪方得到所有人的支持。独立咨询小组建议让居民共同比较多种方案。

起初讨论很紧张，居民怀疑结果早已内定。委员会于是公开支出、收购物价格和租约条款，让大家知道每项决定由谁负责，提升透明度与可信度。

民意调查围绕真实需求展开，主要问题是空间是否负担得起。空楼层用于教学，底层开店；少量拨款支持夜校，其余费用由租金承担。

委员必须解释决定，不能武断制定规则或让单个投资者控制过程。他们同意管理租金、限制突然涨价，并公开搬迁赔偿，接受询问。

最终协议仍未让所有人满意，部分委员对店铺规模有不同意见，但方案有明确框架，居民也确实能参与。委员会承诺每月报告，一年后复查。

公共合作需要时间；未经解释就实施规则，会削弱信任。好的规划要同时考虑建筑和生活在其中的人。

返回对应英文

03 · Reading Beyond a Word List

学期开始时，英语老师问学生，能否不逐词翻译就理解一篇短文。许多人能背长词表，但熟词换个语境就让他们焦虑。

老师解释，词表只呈现部分词义，还要观察相邻词和句中作用。副词会影响对动作的理解，分号能连接紧密相关的意思，这些都不能忽略。

学生阅读学校花园的段落，写总结、比较答案并请求说明。他们要区分作者真正说了什么，以及读者自己想象了什么。能找出来源并纠正的错误猜测，也有学习价值。

词典很有用，但频繁查词打断阅读。老师建议每读完一部分再暂停，收集陌生表达、查阅笔记，同时学习拼读和拼写。读下去不必以发音完美为前提。

另一项练习是复述儿童日记中的经历，检查逻辑是否连贯、是否忠于原文。翻译同样需要认真，可以用简单中文表达，不必照搬英文词序。

老师关注阅读流畅度，但不把它等同于速度。不理解的快读没有意义。通过定期练习养成习惯，能读懂完整论证时，学习会更有意义。

没有魔法公式。坚持、专注、诚实承认薄弱之处，比每天追求完美分数更重要。短时复习能巩固知识，让进步可见，帮助人继续坚持。

返回对应英文

04 · A Farm That Learns from Its Land

村庄遇到更长的干旱期和更难预测的降雨，各块地受影响不同。购买设备前，农民需要弄清差异。

研究人员检查土壤的有机物和养分：一块地缺养分，另一块地因粗砂而失水太快。问题不同，解决方式也应不同。

村庄改变灌溉方式，沿每行植物铺管道，用自动控制按需供水，不必更换全部设备就能应用新方法，并减少浪费；稻草覆盖地面以保持湿润，并避免每年翻动所有土壤。

研究人员比较了生长快和用水少的两个豆类品种。基因能影响生长，但任何植物都受环境限制，良种不能代替合理照料。用水需求预测也只是估计，不是保证。

收获时，孩子在学校花园采收成熟豌豆，向园丁学习移栽。院里有公鸡、鹅和鸽子，他们观察孵化，了解干净水源对动物和作物的重要性。

村庄没有一夜暴富，但生产效率提高、用水成本下降。年度复查帮助预见问题、比较成果。谨慎利用土地所带来的渐进变化，既支持生计，也照顾下一代的需要。

返回对应英文

05 · Making Space for Wildlife

学生来到东北方森林，勘查新公路对野生动物的影响。从八百米海拔望下去，溪流穿过松林，小径通往洞穴。

环保人士解释，道路会将动物栖息地割裂。森林从空中看似完整，地面上却可能互不连通。狐狸能跨小路，高速公路却危险得多，路下隧道可帮助动物穿行。

学生调查蝙蝠、鸟群和迁徙动物。某些动物只在特定季节到来，所以单在夏天统计会遗漏信息。偶尔目击和经常性活动都需要记录。

游客中心的影片展现母老虎觅食，但导游提醒，不能把野生动物当成驯服宠物，不应追赶或触摸。人的侵扰可能吓走动物；学生还查阅了非法狩猎和野生动物交易的报告。

海边渔民介绍船只噪声对海豚的影响，让学生理解保护工作关乎生存条件，而不只是好看的照片。悬崖、泥岸和裸地也可能各有用途。

学生建议保护栖息地间的通道、减少无谓干扰。这不能消除全部问题，但能降低动物的脆弱性。野生动物的消失并非总是不可避免，谨慎行动能够保护它们生活、休息和育幼的地方。

返回对应英文

06 · A Museum of Ordinary Lives

博物馆改展后仍陈列皇家旗帜和国王画像，但也请游客关注王室之外的农民、工人和奴隶。

社会学学者参与策展，展示地区工业化过程：铁匠铺变成机械作坊，纺织企业随后出现。铁制工具与棉布、富人银盘与工人木碗形成对照。

资料介绍农业公社和对强迫劳动的反抗，没有掩饰贫困，也没有歌颂殖民统治，而是说明贵族控制土地、普通人缺少自主权的处境。

政治变化通过个人物品展现：共和国成立宣告、纪念死者的纪念碑，以及共和支持者之间的争论。有些人担心新独裁者取代旧统治者，也有人担心民族主义加深分歧。

破衣、瘦弱儿童的照片和远方亲人的信，让历史变得具体。女继承人捐出遗产中的珍珠项链资助教育，她的慷慨由记录证明，而非家族传说。

展览希望丰富公众理解：欣赏美丽物品，也要追问谁制作了它、付出了什么代价。美丽不能抹去不公。展览最终要让普通生命得到尊重，展示重大历史变化如何影响每个家庭。

返回对应英文

07 · An Investigation Is Not a Verdict

报社收到有关公共工程腐败的举报，称经销商贿赂官员以出售不安全设备。编辑提醒，传闻不是证据。

记者查到无法解释的付款和缺失的安全证明。公司主管否认，称职员偶然出错。记者必须区分疏忽与故意隐瞒。

警方搜查发现涉嫌贿赂的消息，也查到与仓库抢劫有关的另一团伙。报社分开处理，因为人员有联系不等于所有犯罪属于同一计划。

前员工坦白改过数字，其供述很有说服力，但仍需与记录核对。不能仅凭供述指控别人，也不能否认不利于原先判断的证据。

案件进入陪审团审理，一名被告此前获准保释。法庭核查证据有效性和行为人的知情程度。看似确定的证词可能误导，裁决要依据证据，而非公众愤怒。

编辑删去冒犯性描写和辱骂评论。即便行为可耻，也不能据此威胁或拷问。草率报道可能伤害无辜者，即使有罪，也应依法对待。

负责任的报道应揭穿欺骗，同时避免制造新的不公。职业伦理要求记者纠错、保护隐私、抵抗压力，并区分已知事实、争议和不确定信息。

返回对应英文

08 · A Business That Can Keep Its Promises

一名企业家在实习多年后开办维修公司。她并非富翁，积蓄有限，作坊最初只是店后的小房间，当地家庭把能工作的冰箱视为必需品，但新冰箱昂贵；她想提供大家负担得起的维修服务。

她专修家电，也出售修复后的产品。品牌和商标有帮助，但管理能力更重要。她必须控制开支、保持偿付能力，否则一个糟糕月份就可能使企业破产。

她事先说明零件、人工和运费。付款日期可协商，但逾期账单不能永远忽视。面对欠款，她礼貌提醒，并请银行出纳帮助理解账户记录。

海外大订单带来新选择。货物海运、外币收款，因此她核算出口服务和汇率波动风险；忽略这些可能让诱人的订单造成严重损失。

明确的管理规则有用，过度限制却浪费时间。她提拔能干员工，邀请大家参与工具和排班决策，改善配合，增强团队凝聚力。

她不追求每次维修的利润最大化，用适度折扣积累回头客与信任。企业虽小，却因能兑现承诺而前景良好。持久成功不只是销售迅速增长。

返回对应英文

09 · What a Community Kitchen Really Needs

社区食堂想让菜单既实惠又吸引人，提供午餐和外带晚饭。厨师喜欢认真做简单食物，而非罗列昂贵菜品。

早餐有吐司、鸡蛋和水果，午餐有米饭、蔬菜与肉菜。周五可选牛排或鱼，蟹肉偶尔供应。酱汁清淡，胡椒放桌上，让不喜欢咸味的人自行调味。

志愿者称面粉、调柠檬水，为花生酱单独贴标签，切水果并检查果肉。生熟食分开保存，记录冰箱温度，认真冷藏。

清洁规则包括洗手、使用干净毛巾、扫地拖地、分类丢包装、保持炉旁无垃圾，也重视厕所卫生。

减少浪费不能忽略安全：食物会变质，面包会发霉，牛奶会变馊。工作人员不用浓味掩盖可疑食物的气味，也不认定所有防腐剂都有害，而是请专家解释配料与保存要求。

培训兼顾营养和成本，教大家在桌上摆放盘子、均分食物，礼貌对待挑剔顾客。附近咖啡馆的商贩每周捐菜。好饭菜既依赖厨艺，也依赖这种合作。

返回对应英文

10 · A Clinic Built Around People

内陆县城的新诊所配有外科医生、护士和巡诊医生。接待处安静清楚，病人不必反复询问去哪里。

诊所首先重视预防。教育项目讲解传染病、回答艾滋病问题，避免靠恐惧宣传。疾病暴发、信息不清时容易恐慌。告示区分发生率和严重程度：多常见与多严重不是一回事。

患者各有不同情况：工人虚弱发热，伤者疼痛并担心肋骨骨折。医生先检查，再决定住院或准许离开。有人头晕苍白，会得到帮助，而非只被要求等候。

陌生术语会加重焦虑，医生用简单图解释肺和肝脏。专家介绍器官移植，不把它说成万能答案。处方清晰列药，患者可以询问用途；治疗决定应由受训人员作出。

照护也要维护尊严。检查伤疤、清洗伤口前征求同意，理解康复需要时间，也认真听取患者感到不对劲的直觉。

诊所不能消除所有负担，但友善问候、清楚解释、尊重性别和个人处境能够缓解痛苦。冷漠使人无助地等待答复，好的服务让人被倾听。患者感谢工作人员把他们当作具体的人。

返回对应英文

11 · When a Journey Changes Its Plan

一名学生假期游山，买了特快列车单程票，检查护照，轻装出发，并选择靠近车厢门的座位以便照管行李。

站台拥挤，机场接驳车送来航班取消后的乘客。铁路工作人员帮助他们选择替代路线。学生急切想出发，但没有匆忙穿过大门。

节庆使酒店房间紧张，经理提供更远处度假地的临时住处。虽然不太方便，但风景壮丽，学生接受并乘吉普前往木屋。

次晨寒冷多雾，高海拔天气可能迅速恶化，积雪让一段道路危险。导游推迟长途步行，说明勇于冒险不等于忽视风险。

短途路上，学生见到驮物的骡子、南方群山上的云，以及日落时远处的大海。旅途虽偏离计划，景色仍极佳。

导游说，旅游既支持家庭生计，也造成压力。雨季有游客要求不安全的行程。考虑实际条件，调整计划胜于冒险。难忘旅行需要灵活性，不必控制每一步。

返回对应英文

12 · Learning to Train, Learning to Stop

大学体育社团五月迎新，教练强调锻炼要适合个人能力，目标不只是肌肉强壮。学生可选跑步、骑车、游泳，冬季还有滑雪课。

体育场上，大家戴号码标识。第一次训练不为选最强者，而是学习动作与配合，新手跑一圈后休息。教练观察呼吸和感受，不赞扬硬撑疼痛。

一名少年怕显弱，想证明男子气概、击败所有对手，却因跑太快伤踝、头晕。教练停止训练，说明拉伤不是成就，拒绝帮助也不是勇敢。

下周他改变了态度，检查鞋、系好鞋带，练习直立姿态、减少多余动作。面对弹来的球，他后退避让，不再冲撞。小进步带来真实满足。

教练用鼓励激发大家。获胜者应祝贺对手并保持谦逊，可以欢庆，但妒忌与轻蔑会伤害社团。即便失望也要尊重别人。

第九次课时，出勤和自信都有改善。年轻人的活力重要，适度训练与休息同样重要。流汗不等于进步；教练希望学生享受运动，赛后仍继续。

返回对应英文

13 · A Stage Made by Its Community

小剧场为即将到来的节庆排一部音乐剧，讲年轻女子离家又回乡帮助家人的故事。女主由羞怯但愿意练习的学生出演。

吉他手、爵士歌手和退休演员共同参与。器乐开场后是节奏鲜明的歌曲。导演鼓励自然表达和适度即兴，避免脸部动作机械、演出乏味。

后台志愿者用捐来的布做服装：紫色柔软裙子、旧坐垫填充的沙发、剪裁布料与化妆。宽松裤子用于喜剧角色，大外套让演员显老。

导演否决了用“性感”宣传年轻演员的广告，因为它不符合角色，也把作品简化成外貌。新版海报介绍艺术选择，并清楚标示购票安排。

排练时，父亲角色称女主“亲爱的”，最初有些尴尬，通过点头和温暖声调变得可信。无论爱情戏还是家庭戏，都需要信任与相互尊重。

首演高潮是家人重逢。结局有希望但并不完美：问题仍在，人们可以一起面对。戏迷赞赏演出；它不是名作，却让观众享受，也让演员获得持久友谊。

返回对应英文

14 · Why Old Stories Still Matter

图书馆活动中，小说家和诗人讨论旧故事为何仍吸引人。虚构能给困难问题提供想象空间，传说英雄和精灵虽然不真实，感情却可能真实。

小说家的新书讲冒险者穿越沙漠，得到牛仔帮助，又受村中姑娘提醒。“lass”适合故事较古老的叙述语气，不是现代对话中称呼每个年轻女子的普通词。

主人公进入废庙下的地道，遇到巨人和骇人阴影，后来石鸟奇迹般活过来，带他脱险。这是吸引读者的叙事，不是自然科学知识。

诗人追问主人公动机。小说家说，他对认可的渴求逐渐改变：起初想获得至高权力，后来重视仁慈，善待敌人胜过赢得战斗。

不同经历使人对同一谚语或引语作出不同理解，这也是文学魅力。熟悉情景经由陌生声音讲述，可能产生独特之处。

两位作者也讨论修改：删去空话、强化主旨。模仿他人的作品只复制表面，好的创作提供独特视角。故事可以不是真事，却传达关于人的真实理解。

返回对应英文

15 · The Sky Is Not a Simple Picture

天文学家用球形行星模型向公众介绍天空：模型沿轨道运动，较小天体绕它转动，但模型只是思考工具，并非天空的完整复制。

理解行星运动需要观察和计算。小错误可能改变预测位置；几何学研究空间关系，算术与乘法也要检查。立方体帮助比较立体与平面，屏幕上平行的线可能代表距离完全不同的物体。

轨道飞行需要推力来改变运动，工程师要考虑燃料、质量和最大载荷。很少的货物也可能耗资巨大，发射成功只是工作的开始。

其他展示包括穿过玻璃的光束和晶体表面。原子不能简单理解为桌上大模型的缩小版，原子世界需要不同观念。真空装置与气体容器可用于比较受控环境。

观众问到相对论和核能，天文学家说明短讲的局限：图像不能消除概念难点，大约的数字也不能当作精确结果。

孩子问太空旅行会否像航空一样普通。科学家没有作夸张承诺，指出海报上看似简单的航天飞机也涉及数千项决定。科学欢迎好奇心，也要求证据；惊叹引出问题，认真工作帮助回答。

返回对应英文

16 · A Science Room Full of Questions

科学教室欢迎好问的小学生。老师希望实验培养分析习惯，每次活动前都让学生写预期结果及可能推翻预期的证据。

蜡受热熔化，学生比较固态和流动态，观察气泡，记录加热强度和时间。桌上圆形标记规定设备位置，不允许把手指伸进热材料。

老师用模型解释酸碱中和，强调这是化合物知识，不是厨房配方。有些材料有毒，不能舔未知物，也不能无指导地摇晃密闭容器。

地质展示有化石、火山和大陆运动地图，说明今天安静的地方可能有活跃的过去，也引入进化变化，但不认为单件物品能解释一切。

学生用鼠标放大图像、查书目索引、注明引用出处，将专业词的起源和含义用日常语言说明。理解了术语，它才有用。

下课仍有很多问题，这是理想结果。聪明回答受欢迎，但有说服力的解释不能只靠自信。承认未知的谦逊很重要；可能出错是检查的理由，不是停止探索的理由。

17 · Preparing Before the River Rises

河边镇区经历过灾难性洪水，居民仍记得水进家门的恐惧。今年消防队长和救援指挥官在夏季暴风雨前组织演练。

救援人员先集结物资，检查船、无线电和毛毯，清理排水口、修围栏、加固河堤，防止水穿过破损处冲走下面的泥土。

演练教大家给救援艇充气、扣安全搭扣。操作员检查发动机马力和使用限制，司机测试刹车，并准备设备故障时的替代路线。

警报发出清晰指引，人员过桥去高处，车辆在狭窄处缓行，不准堵住紧急入口。团队练习帮助在寒风中发抖的老人。

复盘发现一处社区缺少可靠交通，于是安排额外公交并更新人员名单。志愿者学习接待撤离家庭、为湿衣儿童换穿衣服、请求支援。

演练不能消除全部危险，水位骤涨仍可能造成重灾，但准备能减少混乱、改善应对。紧急时合作胜过夸张承诺。计划必须让人看得懂、做得到，才能救人。

18 · More Than a Line on a CV

院长请毕业生谈工作第一年，有人很快就业，有人仍待业。活动承认职业路径不同，帮助学生不被拒绝摧毁动力。

一位毕业生带来旧公文包和早期申请，承认曾以为成绩好就足够，后来才明白企业也要看实际成果。证书记录学习，却不自动证明实践能力。

学校提供六人项目课程，让不同特长的成员执行明确任务、说清贡献。勤奋者也可能需要明确优先级，善于应变者能在原计划失败时找办法。

课程还教如何建立人际联系：带着具体问题联络别人，而非让陌生人包办一切。研讨会上认识的人可能成为盟友，但关系不能替代技能和诚实劳动。

学生应设想有用项目，而非完美未来。尝试失败不等于人失败。发现缺点、采取下一步并检查效果，能逐渐增强信心。努力结合反馈更有效。

院长提醒不要为理想化成功形象牺牲所有闲暇。休息保存活力，掌握技能需要时间。教育应帮助实现现实目标与建立自信，最值得学习的人未必头衔最显赫。

19 · The Work of Staying Close

父母离婚后，一名年轻女子搬到别的城市，与祖母通信却少探望。祖母喜欢手写信，她便买蓝墨水和信封，认真写信。

起初她只报喜，隐瞒孤独和空虚。祖母真诚回信，谈自己年轻时的悲伤与不安，让她觉得自己并不孤单。

家人终于安排团聚，孙辈带花，侄女带蛋糕。旧照片引出家族往事：祖父穿工装在树下笑，照片褪色，记忆仍鲜明。

女子曾叛逆，与家人争吵，现在感到后悔。祖母宽容，却不假装往事不存在，告诉她可以道歉、也可以从中学习。她流泪，祖母轻抚她的下巴。

灯下，小男孩坐祖父腿上讲笑话。祖父笑后疲倦地叹气，约好今晚讲完故事。孩子又指着衬衫污迹，问是否来自蛋糕。

晚上一切普通：椅下有拖鞋，门边有待洗衣服，却让人平静。女子感激家人的接纳。团聚不是一下解决所有分歧，而是给关系新的机会，并通过认真陪伴表达感谢。

20 · Learning to Read Political Arguments

政治学课堂比较公共服务观点，包括资本主义路径与马克思主义的所有权分析。辩论不考服从，支持某个论点就必须说明理由。

一组讨论面临邻国冲突的国家：代表团与大使商谈联盟，有人认为能防袭击，有人担心被迫参战。协议设退出办法，国际规则也可能约束侵略自由。

另一组研究涉及叛逃与暴乱的历史危机，检查报道依据。秘密报告并非因保密就可信，公众厌恶也不能证明所有指控。

课堂进一步讨论道德：同一行为在不同情境下可能有不同评价，需要考虑宗教、传统和法律，并区分习惯与论证。流行观点可能合理，但流行不证明正确。

看似相似的主张可能证据差异巨大。将其等同，可能使表面公平的辩论反而掩盖重要区别。比较是否有用，需要考察边界。

学生提出讨论规则：不说教、不侮辱、不为和气强求一致。可以表达不受欢迎的意见，也要接受追问。先识别主张，再检查证据，最后判断支持强度，热门议题也能更清晰。

21 · A Room Where Newcomers Belong

社区慈善机构在旧礼堂开夜校，主要服务成年人，包括刚到当地、不熟悉语言的移民。监护人可以带孩子来，降低家长参加的困难。

葡萄酒厂捐款、孤儿院支持办校，志愿者布置教室、张贴告示、准备书籍。组织者提醒，不应把勤恳者想成永不疲倦，服务不能依赖无限无偿劳动。

课程教读写、算术和日常交流，帮助未完成中学教育的人熟悉银行与雇主表格，学习清楚留言，提问不必怕尴尬。

文化活动帮助放松：学生练习筷子、分享地方语言歌曲，也解释饭前祈祷。接纳不要求别人遵从自己的信仰。

学校严肃处理种族歧视和针对学生的迫害。尊重不是只给已经融入者的恩惠；接纳允许人保有身份，同时参与共同活动。

约三个月后，需求使夜课增至三倍，每课仍只收少量费用。门口印着郑重承诺，欢迎愿意尊重他人的学习者。承诺与志愿者奉献让项目有了深厚意义。

22 · A Forum Needs More Than Rules

学生经营维修论坛，原先气氛友善，后来闲言渐渐渗入。一条关于商家的随意评论被热门账号转发，变成全国范围的争论。

他在午餐时偶然听见同学争论，有人气愤，有人惊讶于传播速度。帖子喊着“难以置信”“人人都知道”，却没有证据。依赖重复会让谎言显得可信。

他要求指控提供来源，不准发布私人地址或侵扰生活，也不许威胁。严重辱骂可能导致停用账号；用户不能想当然地认为自己对伤害性帖子不负法律责任。

有人想控制所有讨论，在坏工具照片下讽刺“服务真好”。学生要求说明故障，别攻击其他人。面对带怒气的回应，他仍坚持同一规则。

他把投诉、广告和技术问题分类，认识到大字警告不必然可靠，知名账号也不自动值得信任。来源比粉丝数更重要。

规则会令部分人不满甚至离开，但他明白，无须在完全沉默和胡说之间二选一。可以允许分歧，也要求理由，并在争论变成人身攻击前提醒大家论坛的目的。

23 · Building a Bridge People Can Trust

河流东侧城市需要新桥。旧桥过去只通轻车，如今天天承载公交和卡车。工程师发现木石支撑受损，建议更换。

新桥采用分段建造，将独立部件连接成桥，以减少河上施工时间，也促进工人学习新方法。

开工前工程师登临临时塔顶，检查水深和船只通行空间。工人擦汗讨论重型桥段搬运，要保证起重抓握稳固，避免尖物刺破保护层。

经理不因图纸整齐就认定安全。施工方必须有技能与记录，报告着重列明试验和测量证据。检查员亲自检查连接处，获确认后才放行。

延误无疑会让司机不满，费用也可能增加，但检查不足就提前开放会造成危险。市政府决定继续检测，优先于方便的开通日期。

桥最终改善交通，但工程师不宣称它永久不坏。天气与使用会造成磨损，稳固结构也要定期照管。施工能结束，责任仍继续。

24 · What Cleaner Air Would Actually Mean

北方城市一所学校因连续烟雾弥漫的早晨开展空气项目。街上灰霾笼罩，有人主张马上关闭所有工厂，也有人认为这会终止新增就业计划，却未解决全部成因。

老师让大家思考污染源头，交通、供暖、工业都应检查，汽车产业只是其中一部分。其他大陆的工厂条件不同，照搬外国方案有风险。

学生发起调查，比较不同周的记录。读数略低不证明空气安全，单个数字只提示特定时地的情况，需要了解变化程度。

老师要求去除无依据说法：“明天空气就清新”是愿望，不是预测，污染也不会自动消失。与此同时，他们检查室内情况；门口抽雪茄的人提醒他们，污染也会进入屋内。

最终有两个提案：宣布宏大目标，或说明职责清晰的具体行动。大家选后者。如何兼顾工人生计与清洁生产是棘手问题，需要合作，不能只靠直觉。

学生仍有希望，但既不能漫无目的地等，也不能靠愤怒和确信解决一切。项目的优点是改变了他们追求答案的方式：相信计划会成功之前，先问它需要什么证据。

25 · A Competition That Changed Its Purpose

学生社团办数学奥林匹克赛以激发解题兴趣，这是低年级学生与大学志愿者交流的难得场合，主办者不想让低分变成自我价值判断。

一名男孩熬夜后犯困、走神。志愿者没有训话使他灰心，而是问他对哪道题感兴趣。令人惊讶的是，当他不再感到被盯着时，竟能清楚解释难题。

另一个孩子问是否真由他自己解出，他亲自说明推理。志愿者逐步核查，发现只是一个小错误让他差一点答对。除一人外，大家都接受解释；剩下的问题带来有益讨论。

主办方准备了奖品，参与的志愿者却希望留更多提问时间。个人经验说明，比赛能激励一些人，也会吓退另一些人。主动提问者通常比只等分数者收获多，最有用的问题往往很简单。

社团改变下午安排，让志愿者分站教室两边，方便提问，偶尔允许结伴解题。友善氛围比守住所有原规则重要。

遗憾的是，有些孩子已带着失败感离开。社团邀请他们参加免费后续课，并从失误中学到：最不显眼的收益也可能重要。安静孩子愿意再次提问，成绩表未必能体现，却可能代表希望。下次活动应为此留空间。

巩固重点：先判断句子整体含义，再用核查表确认目标词的具体义项。没有把握的词，返回所在段落重读。
